Reflections suggested by the following passage of Spencer (Note, in the essay “The Ethics of Kant”):

“I am aware that, in the preceding paragraphs, I have been at once unjust and too indulgent toward Kant: unjust, in admitting that his conception of evolution was limited to the genesis of our sidereal system; and too indulgent, in admitting that he had not contradicted himself. My knowledge of Kant’s writings is extremely limited. In 1877, a translation of his Critique of Pure Reason (then recently published, I believe) fell into my hands, and I read a few of the first pages, in which his theory of time and space is set forth; but, as I peremptorily reject it, I closed the book. It has since happened to me twice to do the same, for, being an impatient reader, I find it impossible to go on with a work when I do not agree with the cardinal propositions it contains....”

And elsewhere the same author says that, in cases like the foregoing, he does the author of the book the honor of believing that he will not contradict himself, and, consequently, he does not continue reading, since, being in absolute disagreement with the premises, he must be so with everything that follows from them.

From a magazine article:

“Until very recently it was believed that the smell of things was produced by the diffusion in the air of solid particles detached from them. The theory is very reasonable, though it made people marvel at the extreme divisibility of matter. It is surprising, certainly, that, for instance, a decigram of musk should perfume a room for years and years, and that, analyzed and weighed at the end of them, it should not have lost a quantity appreciable by the most precise balance.

Now then; can odors be determined by a division and expansion of solid matter, as we said at the beginning? According to the English professor of physics, Mr. John Aitken, odoriferous substances strike the olfactory nerves not in solid form, as was hitherto affirmed, but in gaseous form.”

(There follows a summary of experiments to that effect).

“From this it is inferred, consequently, that odors are not subdivided solid matter, but true gaseous emanations.

p. 189

Son interesantes las anteriores observaciones , no sólo desde un punto de vista general, sino desde el especialisimo de la higiene , puesto que vienen á revolucionar ciertas teorias ; por ejemplo , la de que las letrinas son especialmente dañosas por saturar el aire de particulas sólidas descompuestas y mal olientes . Según las teorias del profesor Alkine , el hedor de los albañales , como el aroma de las flores y de los perfumes , es gaseoso, y por tanto nada perju-diciales para la salud si no se respiran en cantidades excesivas . >>> ( Malisimo razonamiento , el del último párrafo : de que las ema-naciones no sean dañosas por ser particulas sólidas, no se sigue que no lo sean de ningún modo; podrán serlo por otra causa. Notar, además , cómo pretende imponerse el raciocinio en un caso que debe ser ante todo experimental. Véase , pues , que importante será esa función, cuando sólo á ella responde todo el inmenso cambio del polluelo al gallo .... etc » . En una clase, como muy adecuados ejemplos de simplismo en el pensamiento y falsa precisión, incomprensión por falta de sim- 13.

The foregoing observations are interesting not only from a general point of view, but from the very special one of hygiene, since they come to revolutionize certain theories; for example, the theory that latrines are especially harmful because they saturate the air with decomposed and foul-smelling solid particles. According to the theories of Professor Alkine, the stench of sewers, like the aroma of flowers and of perfumes, is gaseous, and therefore not at all harmful to health if they are not breathed in excessive quantities.”

(A very bad piece of reasoning, that of the last paragraph: from the fact that emanations are not harmful as solid particles, it does not follow that they are not harmful in any way; they may be so for another reason. Notice, besides, how reasoning tries to impose itself in a case that must be above all experimental. And the possible illusion of not realizing that it is reasoning, and of believing that this consequence is justified by the experiments.... etc.)

Logical horror (from a newspaper article):

“Milk should never be kept in clear vessels, for light alters it considerably. The irrationality of the practice, general today, of giving the feeding bottle in translucent flasks is shown by Nature herself. The suckling infant or the young animal takes the milky juice directly from the mother, that is, without the milk being exposed even for a moment to the altering action of light.”

Cuestión ( que tomo de una correspondencia publicada en un diario) : « ¿ Puede ser literatura el periodismo ? » Pensar lo que puede haber de hecho, en esa cuestión , y lo que es de palabras . En la pág. 71 de la presente obra, hay un ejemplo exagerado (que no pude resistir á la tentación de poner). Efecto lógico y psi-cológico , que produce.

of criticisms made on an inferior mental plane, of the wrong way of treating questions of degree, and of fallacies of degree, etc., read and have analyzed passages from Nordau’s work “Degeneration.” Very striking examples in themselves, and still more so because the author is (and this will also be brought out by readings) a penetrating, brilliant and agile mind, original, independent, etc.

Give readings from Spencer, as very striking examples of false or excessive systematization (his great system is a general example, and the partial ones are innumerable); of frequent incomprehension owing to closed states of mind, to narrow hostility toward other opinions, etc. (For example: the discussion of idealism, in the Principles of Psychology, Vol. II; his attitude toward Kant, etc.). A very notable case: when (in the Principles of Sociology) his theory of the “social organism” would lead him logically to socialism, and, by inconsistent verbal distinctions, he defends himself against it. Etc., etc.

These are the good examples, because they show how great all those dangers are, since, even where minds of genius are concerned, they produce such an effect.

The work of Spencer may be compared with that of Mill. (Readings from both.) The forced systematization of the former, and its false precision, which give it a more considerable appearance before the public, facilitate its popularization, etc. (How Spencer’s work can be summed up, and not Mill’s.) Greater sincerity with himself on Mill’s part; greater depth properly so called, owing to the share of doubt, ignorance, and even contradiction in his soul.

(Lógicamente, si no refuerza ni motiva, á causa de su carác-ter excepcional, lo que alli se explica, por lo menos tampoco lo debilita ; pero psicológicamente, puede debilitarlo : entender por qué.) In a discussion on the moral effect of education:

A.—The beneficial effect of education is indisputable. It is enough to observe the scant morality of the uneducated classes. Although the morality of the educated classes may not be worth much, that of the uneducated is worth still less.

B.—I do not deny that fact. But, precisely because they are more educated, people of the upper classes are more worthy of censure and more blameworthy... etc.

Appraise B.’s answer. (Bad reasoning: deviation from the question.)

Examples for appreciating the difference between thinking by systems and thinking by ideas to be taken into account; of the reconciliation of opposed theses; of questions of degree, etc.:

1

A.—The more one is made to study in surface (increase of subjects and syllabi), the less in depth...

B.—Studying more makes one more intelligent, broadens horizons, etc., and makes it possible to know each thing better, even though more things are studied. The mind must not be regarded as a receptacle of fixed capacity... etc.

2

A.—It is useless to create the posts (for example: many primary schools; a Faculty of Philosophy in a country of little culture) if one does not have people capable of filling them properly... etc.

B.—By creating the posts, the men end by appearing, or by being formed... Just as the function creates the organ... etc.

Notice, too, in either of those two examples, how the

En una ampliación posterior, usé , entre otros casos tomados de la realidad, éste : Un conocidono dispeptico, y muy aficionado à tratar por si mismo sus dolencias , me contaba un dia que, según las revistas y libros que él leia, dos teorías generales sobre las enfermedades digestivas , se disputaban entonces la boga en el mundo cientifico : una, que buscaba la causa de muchas de ellas en la estrechez del piloro ; otra, la de Mechnikoff, basada en la flora microbiana del intestino grueso ; y que , en cada una de estas teorias, se basaba un tratamiento . -¿Y por cuál de los dos se ha decidido Vd. ?, le pregunté : -Yo me aplico los dos . Have the students think rightly (more or less) in each of those cases.

Look for examples of false precision in investigations by the method of questionnaires with a great number of questions, from which averages are afterwards drawn, etc.

The teacher will very easily find examples—some of them incredible— by looking for them, above all, in the early period of laboratory psychology (more especially, works from the German and North American laboratories).

The teacher can present many extreme and brutal examples of false opposition in the “Futurism” of the man of letters Marinetti.

The teacher, in connection with false opposition, may introduce (and it will be good, didactically, for him to do so) examples of oppositions that are not false (because some students are apt to become confused, and tend to believe that all oppositions are false, or that more oppositions are false than really are). Also (important), cases of oppositions legitimate up to a certain point and false beyond it, or legitimate from a certain point of view and false from another, etc.

I forgot these exercises in the lessons that were taken down in shorthand to form the text. In a later expansion I used, among other cases taken from reality, this one:

An acquaintance of mine, a dyspeptic, and very fond of treating his ailments himself, told me one day that, according to the magazines and books he read, two general theories of digestive diseases were then vying for favor in the scientific world: one, which sought the cause of many of them in narrowness of the pylorus; the other, Metchnikoff’s, based on the microbial flora of the large intestine; and that on each of these theories a treatment was based.

—And which of the two have you decided on? I asked him:

—I apply both to myself.

In this case, one might presume both treatments to be incompatible; at least, it was neither reasonable nor prudent to suppose them

p. 193

complementarios ni siquiera no contradictorios : la oposición podía muy bien no ser falsa : ser legitima. Mi interlocutor cometia , ó podia cometer, el paralogismo contrario al común . Las demostraciones de « Los Primeros Principios de Spencer, procuran probar que el Universo tiende , en todo momento , á pasar à un estado más heterogéneo , coherente , definido , etc., y ese cam-bio constituye la « evolución » . Después , se trata de la « disolución » ; y se prueba que, llegado el universo al fin de la evolución , ten-derá à recorrer á la inversa todos los grados , y á volver hacia la homogeneidad .... Resúmanse ó mejor, léanse) esos raciocinios, y muéstrese lo siguiente : Los « raciocinios » , en uno y otro caso, parecen convincentes al autor, y, probablemente, al lector no prevenido . Entre tanto, si suponemos (como lo hace el autor al universo pasando, en regre-sión, por un estado por el cual pasó ya en evolución , resulta que hemos probado, para un mismo estado , que tiende à la heterogenei-dad (primera demostración) y que tiende á la homogeneidad ( se-gunda demostración .... Mostrar con este ejemplo ( además de las traiciones del racio-cinio puro ), cómo sólo el estado de espiritu creado por la falsa sistematización, puede hacer fácil que escapen á un gran pensador estas dificultades . Razones para : A. Comer, en cada comida, un solo plato , hasta saciarse ( sólo trabajan ciertos elementos digestivos , y descansan los otros ... etc .. B. - Comer la mayor variedad posible (ninguno se cansa dema- siado ; trabajan todos ... etc.) . (Utilizar este ejemplo como otros anteriores análogos . complementary, nor even non-contradictory: the opposition might very well not be false: it might be legitimate. My interlocutor was committing, or might be committing, the paralogism contrary to the common one.

The demonstrations of Spencer’s “First Principles” seek to prove that the Universe tends, at every moment, to pass into a more heterogeneous, coherent, definite state, etc., and that this change constitutes “evolution.” Afterwards “dissolution” is dealt with; and it is proved that, the universe having reached the end of evolution, it will tend to run through all the degrees in reverse, and to return toward homogeneity.... Summarize (or better, read) those reasonings, and show the following:

The “reasonings,” in the one case and in the other, seem convincing to the author, and, probably, to the unwary reader. Meanwhile, if we suppose (as the author does) the universe passing, in regression, through a state through which it has already passed in evolution, it turns out that we have proved, for one and the same state, that it tends toward heterogeneity (first demonstration) and that it tends toward homogeneity (second demonstration)....

Show by this example (besides the treacheries of pure reasoning) how only the state of mind created by false systematization can make it easy for these difficulties to escape a great thinker.

Reasons for:

A.—Eating, at each meal, a single dish, until satisfied (only certain digestive elements work, and the others rest... etc.).

B.—Eating the greatest possible variety (none gets too tired; they all work... etc.).

(Use this example like the analogous earlier ones. And have the students imagine what a well-conducted discussion on this point would be like, and what a badly conducted discussion would be like. It may be the subject of a written exercise).

Use this other one like the preceding example:

A.—Tolstoy’s extreme thesis (taken from the Gospel), of never using violence, not even to restrain violence.

B.—The opposite one.

Otro ejemplo : A.- Seguir siempre la moda en los vestidos ( razones más admi-sibles : no singularizarse en cosas de poca monta ; no ofender el gusto del prójimo , que se acostumbra á las modas, etc. ) . B. Prescindir en absoluto de las modas . Otro ejemplo : A.- Intransigencia absoluta contra los saludos y otras fórmulas de urbanidad prácticas cuákeras ; origen de los saludos , que serian restos del homenaje de los vasallos , etc. ) . B.- La tendencia extrema opuesta . Ejemplo de cuestión vaga ( las de que se habla en las páginas 76 , 77 ), é inaceptable tal como se la plantea ( la he visto discutir, con el resultado que puede imaginarse ) : « ¿ Qué es más fuerte : la herencia ó la influencia del medio ? » Un autor ( Herzen : « Le Cerveau » .) combate la « libertad » . Pone esta nota : <<Una singular definición de la libertad , que nunca he podido comprender, es la que da el señor Delbœuf..... « Ser libre , no es obrar por si, ni pensar los motivos ; es simple-mente suspender nuestra respuesta á la solicitación, dejar para otro momento nuestra decisión, esperar asi la producción de otros motivos . La elección es , pues , motivada, pero los motivos dejan de ser determinantes , en el sentido de que el ser libre « pone un intervalo de tiempo entre la idea del acto y el acto » . No es ya la libertad de acción ; ¡ es la libertad de inacción ! Es completamente como si se dijera : la función de los centros moto-res no consiste en producir el movimiento con tal ótal grupo muscular, dejando á los otros grupos en reposo ; consiste en im-pedir el movimiento que tiende sin cesar á producirse en todos los músculos, y á no permitirlo más que en tal ó tal grupo mus-cular determinado . Es la apoteosis de la inhibición , convengo en ello ; pero no veo en qué, eso , modifica la eficacia determinante de los moti-vos ... etc .>>> Hacer buscar por los estudiantes todo lo que haya de defectuoso lógicamente en ese pasaje ; por ejemplo : 1.° La comparación defectuosa , que introduce nuevos elementos de absurdo , etc.

Another example:

A.—Always follow the fashion in dress (most admissible reasons: not to make oneself conspicuous in trifling matters; not to offend the taste of one’s neighbor, who grows accustomed to the fashions, etc.).

B.—Dispense with fashions altogether.

Another example:

A.—Absolute intransigence against greetings and other formulas of civility (Quaker practices; origin of greetings, which would be remnants of the homage of vassals, etc.).

B.—The opposite extreme tendency.

Example of a vague question (of the kind spoken of on pages 76, 77), and unacceptable as it is posed (I have seen it discussed, with the result that may be imagined):

“Which is stronger: heredity or the influence of the environment?”

An author (Herzen: “Le Cerveau.”) combats “freedom.” He puts this note:

“A singular definition of freedom, which I have never been able to understand, is the one given by M. Delbœuf.....

“To be free is not to act of oneself, nor to weigh the motives; it is simply to suspend our response to the solicitation, to put off our decision to another moment, thus to await the production of other motives. The choice is, then, motivated, but the motives cease to be determining, in the sense that the free being ‘puts an interval of time between the idea of the act and the act.’

This is no longer freedom of action; it is freedom of inaction! It is exactly as though one were to say: the function of the motor centers does not consist in producing movement with such-and-such a muscle group, leaving the other groups at rest; it consists in preventing the movement that tends incessantly to be produced in all the muscles, and in permitting it only in such-and-such a determinate muscle group.

It is the apotheosis of inhibition, I grant; but I do not see in what way that modifies the determining efficacy of motives... etc.”

Have the students look for everything that is logically defective in that passage; for example:

1st. The defective comparison, which introduces new elements of absurdity, etc.

p. 195

2. ° La actitud de hostilidad, revelada en ciertos giros de la ex-presión. 3.º ( Muy interesante ) : el poder de las palabras ( « libertad de inacción » , con su signo de exclamación ; « apoteosis de la inhibi-ción» , etc. ) , para producir, psicológicamente, y sin fundamento lógico alguno , impresión desfavorable à la teoria criticada, en el ánimo del lector que no esté especialmente prevenido . Nota. Es muy conveniente mostrar á veces todas estas cosas , y lo demás del mismo orden, en pasajes como éste de Herzen , en que no están demasiado visibles , ni aparecen en grado extremo , sino algo velados, no muy claros ni evidentes ... Un ejercicio utilisimo : mostrar pasajes en que se defienda una tesis buena con malos argumentos . Mostrar lo que suele suceder en esos casos . En una discusión : Uno de los interlocutores sostiene que se debe admitir la belleza en todas sus formas; poder gustar de todos los estilos , deleitarse con todas las obras ..... ¿Aún con las obras malas ? No : con las malas , no . ¿Y cómo se sabe cuáles son las malas ? (Esto último , en la mente del que hace la pregunta, va como objeción contra la tesis del otro . Reflexiones .) Una clase de cuestiones vanas : Los hombres empiezan por crear dos entidades, con palabras; después , buscan las diferencias , y van probando que cada diferencia que se propone no es com-pleta ó absoluta, etc. Ejercicios académicos muy comunes ; por ejem-plo : diferencia entre la moral y el derecho » ; otros , innumerables ( sobre todo en los estudios juridicos , sociológicos , etc. ) . ¿Habrá , ó no, falsa oposición en este pasaje ? «Dejando á los historiadores de la filosofia la tarea de consignar las suposiciones de los lógicos formalistas acerca de la manera como pensariamos correctamente ( si ello fuera posible ); nosotros

2nd. The attitude of hostility, revealed in certain turns of expression.

3rd. (Very interesting): the power of words (“freedom of inaction,” with its exclamation mark; “apotheosis of inhibition,” etc.) to produce, psychologically, and without any logical foundation, an unfavorable impression of the theory criticized in the mind of the reader who is not especially forewarned.

Note.—It is very advisable to show at times all these things, and the rest of the same order, in passages like this one of Herzen’s, in which they are not too visible, nor appear in an extreme degree, but somewhat veiled, not very clear or evident...

A most useful exercise: show passages in which a good thesis is defended with bad arguments. Show what usually happens in such cases.

In a discussion:

One of the interlocutors maintains that one ought to admit beauty in all its forms; to be able to enjoy all styles, to delight in all works.....

—Even in bad works?

—No: not in the bad ones.

—And how does one know which are the bad ones?

(This last, in the mind of the one who asks the question, is meant as an objection against the other’s thesis. Reflections.)

A class of idle questions: Men begin by creating two entities, with words; afterwards they look for the differences, and go on proving that each difference proposed is not complete or absolute, etc. Very common academic exercises; for example: “difference between morality and law”; others, innumerable (above all in juridical, sociological studies, etc.).

Is there, or is there not, false opposition in this passage?

On mental “planes”: those states through which young people are apt to pass, for example with regard to “Science,” or to a certain literature or forms of style of excessive romanticism, etc. The superiority they feel, and how they confuse those who are above that mental state with those who are below it.

A Pedagogist maintains that the State has the right at any moment to make teachers in service sit an examination, and that this obligation should be laid down for them in the regulations: “If the State has the right, on conferring a post on a certain person, to verify whether he has the necessary knowledge, it also has the right, in order to keep him in the post, to verify whether he retains it...”

Value of that argument, and discussion of the point.

An article begins thus: “Does the Belgian literary soul exist?”

Considerations on what that article may be like, or on what may happen in discussing that question.

In our secondary education, examinations used to be oral.

A project was presented to make those in Grammar, Literature, Philosophy, History and some other subject written.

Among the teachers, in the newspapers, etc., a great discussion arose.

(Let the teacher present the arguments, if he knows this discussion; if not, he will himself easily formulate the principal ones on either side.)

Now then: the discussion, as it took place among us (it can be seen in the “Anales de la Universidad de Montevideo”) was an excellent example of the usual bad way of treating normative problems.

Recorte de un diario : LAS NUEVAS TEORÍAS DE LA MÚSICA « La música es el acento de la palabra . <<<De esta manera acaba de condensar sus teorias musicales el <<critico hoy de moda en Paris , Raúl Gunsbourg . <<Es por haber desconocido esta verdad, ha agregado, es por ha- << ber permanecido ciego ante ese huevo de Colón, que, durante si- <<glos, la humanidad ha errado en un laberinto sin salida. Mientras « que todas las otras artes han fijado lo bello que tiene carácter <«> eterno » , sólo la música ha permanecido imprecisa y, con raras « excepciones , efimera . <<Dirigiéndose únicamente a los sentidos del espectador, la fuerza <<vital de la música, contenia, al mismo tiempo, el gérmen de su <<muerte . Cada uno, escuchando los sonidos musicales , ponia en « ellos un algo de su cerebro y de su alma, y en ellos veía y oía el <<el propio eco de su corazón a menudo ulcerado, la propia ilusión <<de su cerebro muchas veces alterado .

But it was also an example of an enormous and curious error: not one of those of us who took part in the discussion then, whether for or against the bill, realized that the real question to be resolved was not whether written examinations were preferable to oral ones or oral ones to written, but this other one (which could be prepared for by discussing the first, but was not the same): whether it was preferable for all examinations to be oral, or for some to be oral and others written.

A friend of mine was complaining about the present difficulties of domestic service: “those servants bound to the household, who lived and died in it, no longer exist,” etc.

I answered that this was, to my mind, the beginning of a social good, for the domestic servant must disappear, since that constant dependence, at every moment, that permanent subordination of one human being to another, is against nature and inhuman, as is living in another’s home, whether by renouncing the founding of one’s own, or by abandoning it.... etc.

He raised the following objection:

“But dependence exists in every profession! A physician or a lawyer depends on his clients; an employee, on his superiors.... etc., etc.”

Value of that objection.

Clipping from a newspaper:

The New Theories of Music

“Music is the accent of the word.

“Thus has Raoul Gunsbourg, the critic now in fashion in Paris, just condensed his musical theories.

“It is through having failed to recognize this truth, he has added, it is through having remained blind before this egg of Columbus, that for centuries humanity has wandered in a labyrinth with no way out. While all the other arts have fixed the beautiful that has an ‘eternal character,’ music alone has remained imprecise and, with rare exceptions, ephemeral.

“Addressing itself solely to the senses of the listener, the vital force of music contained at the same time the germ of its death. Each person, listening to the musical sounds, put into them something of his brain and of his soul, and in them saw and heard the very echo of his often ulcerated heart, the very illusion of his often disordered brain.

“And when, with time, the banality, the nullity and the uselessness of those sounds became apparent, one felt shame for the feelings that had been prostituted.

“The old definitions of music that incomprehension has sought, ‘an infinite dream,’ ‘a world of sorrows and of joys,’ are nothing but the childish words of impotent invalids, repeated by the cohort of those who gather around the incurables at theosophical miracles. In the same symphony, in the same orchestral piece, each listener, reflecting his own soul, heard and saw a different image; the same piece represented for one a storm, for another a sunrise, for a third a battle and for a fourth a hymn of love!

“After a certain time, unless one were incapable, each person was surprised to see in the same piece nothing but a series of banal sonorities.

“And yet music had its mission. It was to be, and it must be, ‘the accent of the word’! An accent that should make but a single body with the word, forming but one indissoluble unity. Without being able to explain how, the public has found its road to Damascus, and, while celebrating one thing or another according to the taste or the fashion of the day, it remains attached nonetheless, in spite of itself, to the only works in which music, being nothing but the harmonious accent of the word, has produced immortal masterpieces.

“It is good form to go into ecstasies over such-and-such a work in which the symphony predominates; but, for oneself, for one’s own enjoyment, one goes twenty times to hear ‘Carmen’ or some other work of pure melody, because in them word and music are but one.

“That duet of the fourth act of ‘Carmen,’ in which Don José sings:

“Mais moi, Carmen, je t’aime encore; “Carmen, hélas, moi, je t’adore.” [But I, Carmen, I love you still; Carmen, alas, I adore you.] “.... is melody and is drama, and it is impossible to conceive the phrase with any sounds other than those Bizet put into it. And so it continues, precise and melodious, throughout the whole length of the duet in which the anguished drama unfolds.

“The same may be said of the invocation of Iphise in ‘Dardanus,’ by the first dramatic composer, Rameau.

“Par l’effort de votre art terrible.” [By the effort of your terrible art.]

“This invocation is so just that a hundred and thirty years later Wagner will make use of it, making it the principal leit motiv of the Tetralogy and bringing it back a thousand times in his work.

“If in the dramatic domain there has arisen, in this sense, a genius, Mussorgsky, especially in ‘Boris Godunov,’ we see another genius, an eminently comic one, Rossini, who, through having given just accents to the characters of Beaumarchais’s ‘Barber,’ through having possessed that gift, simple in appearance but supernatural in reality, has made centuries laugh with his ‘Barber of Seville.’

“So strong for the public is the influence of a just and melodious accentuation, that many pages endowed with that divine gift will remain forever engraved in its memory, like Gluck’s ‘J’ai perdu mon Eurydice’ [I have lost my Eurydice], and Donna Anna’s imprecations and the entrance of the Commendatore in Mozart’s ‘Don Juan.’

“A German polyphonic giant, Wagner, has attempted to replace the immortal accent of the word by a polyphonic torrent. He has given the orchestra a preponderant role. Everything that lives and suffers in his drama moans in his orchestra. And yet his art, for the most part, will be ephemeral. Today this one, tomorrow that one, will rival the colossus in polyphonic ‘tour de force,’ and if they do not manage to submerge him, they will succeed in wounding him with his own weapons.

“Always, or almost always, in Wagner, the orchestra creates the atmosphere precisely, but always too, or almost always, the singing has no relation whatever to the word, because it is there only as a counterpoint necessary to the unity of the orchestral polyphony.

“However much one may torture the imagination, it is the canticle of ‘Spring’ and the ride that will immortalize the Valkyries, and it is the song of the ‘Rhine Maidens’ that will remain in the memory of men. The polyphonic monster understood this so well that, when divine inspiration gives him a just and harmonious accent, he makes a ‘leit motiv’ of it, in the hope that, through the instruments, those accents will dominate the word. He is mistaken; his ‘leit motivs’ neither move nor fill the soul except at the precise moment when they take on body with the phrase for which they were created: the rest is ephemeral.

“The error lies in believing that music is an art that can suffice through the agglomeration of sounds. When it is merely symphonic, it can give the work an appearance, but it cannot breathe into it a life of its own, a real existence.

“No one is or can be a musician except one who can find the accents of a harmonic and melodious beauty and express all the nuances of the work through his phrases. Nothing is or can be music except inspired, pure melody, which adapts itself so closely to the word that, once heard, it can no longer be separated from it. And for this no science and no study suffice: Inspiration is necessary.”

“This is what the new critic in vogue has just said in ‘Le Figaro,’ and it shows how, in the great musical centers, Wagnerian fanaticism is dissolving and there is a reaction toward a healthy eclecticism that leads to recognizing in each one the merit and the value that belong to him, placing at the same height schools and musicians that claimed to be on different planes....”

Get the students to discover, each time it occurs, the false opposition in the theories of this critic.

Let them notice, in passing, the absurdity of the journalist’s comment (the final paragraph), in which an accurate consideration (interpreting the foregoing as contrary to exclusive Wagnerism) is mixed with a false one (interpreting it as a case of eclecticism, of non-exclusivism, etc., when it is precisely an exclusive and one-sided theory...).

One speaker advocates the founding of secondary schools for girls.

The other answers:

—That is not the fundamental thing. If in our country women are Catholic, it is because that is how men prefer them, even the liberals. The freethinking woman has little chance of marrying. If tomorrow the great majority of the young men who count most from the matrimonial point of view were to desire, seek, and accept only freethinking women, they would all manage to become so. Consequently, what must be attacked is not the soul of the woman but that of the man.... etc.

(Whatever false opposition there may be in that.)

Note (applicable to many of these examples).—Sophisms, isolated in this way, as I present them here for brevity’s sake, will be perceived with relative ease; but in a class, for example, they will be discovered much less easily if the teacher imagines a long discussion in which these sophisms appear and pass by mixed in with everything else and with nothing special to draw attention to them, as happens in reality.

Traveling on a streetcar, I heard the man next to me on the bench ask someone he was talking to:

“Don’t you think that variety arises from progress?” And they began a discussion on the subject. (A basis of truth in that proposition: but not enough precision in it, probably, to warrant the discussion. Imagine what will happen if that discussion is undertaken without further distinctions or clarifications.)

Afterwards they went on to discuss whether beauty, too, was based on variety; and one of them said that on country estates only deciduous trees ought to be planted....

One of our writers maintained in his books that the bad tendency (then verbalist and “bookish,” according to him) of our secondary and higher education could only be counteracted, remedied, or prevented by a good primary education.

In a discussion of these ideas, it was said that this author was committing the same bad reasoning as the homeopaths, for whom remedies are good in small quantities and become bad in larger quantities.

Un estudio sobre patologia psico - sexual , termina asi : <<En la formación genética de las funciones de reproducción pueden distinguirse tres procesos fisiopsiquicos distintos , adquiridos en el curso de la experiencia de la especie ó del individuo: una emo-ción, una tendencia y un sentimiento, que en el lenguaje usual se designan como voluptuosidad, instinto y amor. La emoción sexual ( voluptuosidad es la reacción psiccorgánica á las excitaciones sensitivas especializadas en los órganos diferen-ciados para la función reproductora heterosexual . La tendencia serual ( instinto es un hábito fijado hereditaria-mente, como producto de la experiencia de la emoción sexual en la evolución filogenética . El sentimiento serual ( amor es una formación psíquica de la experiencia individual sobre la base de la tendencia especifica, siendo una superestructura psicológica útil para la elección que precede á la conjunción. Estas tres manifestaciones de la actividad psicosexual pueden perturbarse conjuntamente ó por separado ; en general puede esta-blecerse cuál de ellas está primitivamente perturbada, pudiendo considerarse morbida toda emoción , tendencia ó sentimiento que no responde al cumplimiento de su finalidad biológica : la reproduc-ción de la especie . Por su orden genético esos procesos constituyen tres grupos : 1.º, Patología del instinto serual ( adquirido en la experiencia de la especie ) , cuyas manifestaciones pueden reunirse con el nombre de <<sexualidad morbida » ; 2.°, Patología de la emotividad sexual ( adquirida en la experiencia individual que llamamos « sensuali-dad morbida » ; 3. °, Patología del sentimiento serual ( adquirido en la evolución individual como representación psicológica de ambas experiencias que llamaremos sentimentalidad mórbida » . Cada uno de ellos puede estar exagerado , atenuado ó desviado . Esta nueva clasificación, fundada en la diferenciación de los tres procesos heterogéneos que componen las funciones psico-sexuales , nos permite separar en tres grupos sus manifestaciones patológicas , confundidas hasta hoy en las descripciones de los psiquiatras y médicos legistas . La atonia del instinto, la anafrodisia sensual y la anestesia mo-ral, por una parte ; - la exaltación instintiva, la hiperestesia sen-sual y la erotomanía sentimental , por otra ; y , por fin, la anomalia del instinto , la perversión sensual y la desviación psíquica son grupos de fenómenos perfectamente distintos é inconfundibles . Este nuevo concepto sintético de la psicopatologia sexual es el único que se armoniza con los datos actuales de la psicologia genética.>>> Estudio lógico de esos pasajes. Si podrá haber ahi algo de falsa precisión ; si el concepto de las clasificaciones , y empleo de esa clasificación , son completamente legitimos y justos , etc.

and as an argument (which is extremely complicated, all the more so since the reasoning to which the comparison is opposed has bad aspects, which makes the objection feel sound, etc....).

A study on psycho-sexual pathology ends thus:

“In the genetic formation of the functions of reproduction three distinct physio-psychical processes may be distinguished, acquired in the course of the experience of the species or of the individual: an emotion, a tendency, and a sentiment, which in ordinary language are designated as voluptuousness, instinct, and love.

The sexual emotion (voluptuousness) is the psycho-organic reaction to the sensory excitations specialized in the organs differentiated for the heterosexual reproductive function.

The sexual tendency (instinct) is a habit fixed hereditarily, as a product of the experience of the sexual emotion in phylogenetic evolution.

The sexual sentiment (love) is a psychical formation of individual experience on the basis of the specific tendency, being a useful psychological superstructure for the choice that precedes conjunction.

These three manifestations of psychosexual activity may be disturbed jointly or separately; in general it can be established which of them is primarily disturbed, and every emotion, tendency, or sentiment that does not serve the fulfillment of its biological end—the reproduction of the species—may be considered morbid.

By their genetic order these processes constitute three groups: 1st, Pathology of the sexual instinct (acquired in the experience of the species), whose manifestations may be brought together under the name of “morbid sexuality”; 2nd, Pathology of sexual emotivity (acquired in individual experience), which we call “morbid sensuality”; 3rd, Pathology of the sexual sentiment (acquired in individual evolution as a psychological representation of both experiences), which we shall call “morbid sentimentality.” Each of them may be exaggerated, attenuated, or deviated.

This new classification, founded on the differentiation of the three heterogeneous processes that make up the psychosexual functions, enables us to separate into three groups their pathological manifestations, confused until now in the descriptions of psychiatrists and medico-legal experts.

Atony of the instinct, sensual anaphrodisia, and moral anesthesia, on the one hand;—instinctive exaltation, sensual hyperesthesia, and sentimental erotomania, on the other;—and, finally, anomaly of the instinct, sensual perversion, and psychical deviation, are perfectly distinct groups of phenomena that cannot be confused.

This new synthetic conception of sexual psychopathology is the only one that harmonizes with the present data of genetic psychology.”

Logical study of these passages. Whether there may be something of false precision in them; whether the conception of the classifications, and the use of that classification, are entirely legitimate and sound, etc.

p. 202

Es utilisimo acostumbrarse á analizar la propiedad de las com-paraciones en las discusiones : generalmente son parcialmente adecuadas y parcialmente inadecuadas; mucho depende de la bue-na voluntad, consciente ó inconsciente , de tomarlas sólo en lo ade-cuado, ó de un acuerdo tácito que en tal sentido se produce en el caso ; de todos modos , es muy conveniente procurar aclarar y dis-cernir ( hasta donde se pueda, porque es muy dificil ) lo que, en esas comparaciones , es adecuado é inadecuado lógicamente, asi como distinguir el valor lógico de la comparación , de esos efectos psico-lógicos, reales ó posibles .... Algunas de las comparaciones cita-das en esta obra pueden servir para ese ejercicio. He aquí (inclui-da en una discusión ), otra para analizar ( no hago ese análisis , porque sólo el sugerirlo ligeramente necesitaría muchas páginas ) . < -<< Usted sostiene que deben permitirse á la mujer todas las car-reras del hombre ; sin embargo, no podrá negar que la mujer, aún sin hablar de la inferioridad de condiciones en que la colocan el embarazo y la maternidad, es menos inteligente que el hombre . -No sé si es menos inteligente. Pero , aún admitido que pueda serlo : la presunción de que no pueda llegar á las más altas cum-bres , no es razón para prohibirle la ascensión . Que llegue hasta donde pueda ! -Pero es que se cae, y se rompe la cabeza! .... y como esto interesa á la sociedad, tiene derecho de impedirlo .... » . Etc. Problema lógico : qué valor tiene en general, y qué valor puede tener en diferentes casos , etc. , esa objeción que se hace contra ciertas teorias ú opiniones , muy comúnmente ( en esta obra hay un ejemplo , pag . 123). « Esa teoria es peligrosa : una vez que se le admita, no se sabe donde detenerse , y uno se desliza ... etc . » . En un concurso de oposición , cometió uno de los opositores una inmoralidad que se comprobó ( fraude, mentira). Alguien sostuvo en el Tribunal, que no se podía tener en cuenta ese hecho, porque los concursos sólo son para comprobar las aptitudes y conocimientos , esto es : lo que sea de orden intelectual, pero no lo que sea de orden moral . (Esta afirmación , no autorizada por ninguna disposición legal, y que es evidentemente mala en sus consecuencias, había sido va-gamente sugerida por la falacia de que se habla en la pág. 59:

It is extremely useful to get into the habit of analyzing the aptness of the comparisons made in discussions: generally they are partly adequate and partly inadequate; much depends on the good will, conscious or unconscious, to take them only in what is adequate in them, or on a tacit agreement to that effect that comes about in the particular case; in any event, it is very advisable to try to clarify and discern (as far as one can, for it is very difficult) what in such comparisons is logically adequate and inadequate, as well as to distinguish the logical value of the comparison from its psychological effects, real or possible.... Some of the comparisons cited in this work may serve for that exercise. Here (included in a discussion) is another one to analyze (I do not make that analysis, because merely to suggest it lightly would require many pages).

—“You maintain that women should be allowed to enter all the careers open to men; and yet you will not be able to deny that woman, even leaving aside the inferiority of conditions in which pregnancy and motherhood place her, is less intelligent than man.

—I do not know whether she is less intelligent. But, even granting that she may be: the presumption that she may not be able to reach the highest summits is no reason to forbid her the ascent. Let her go as far as she can!

—But the trouble is that she falls, and breaks her head!.... and since this concerns society, society has the right to prevent it....”. Etc.

Logical problem: what value has in general, and what value may have in different cases, etc., that objection which is very commonly made against certain theories or opinions (there is an example in this work, page 123): “That theory is dangerous: once it is admitted, one does not know where to stop, and one slides... etc.”.

In a competitive examination for a post, one of the candidates committed an immoral act which was proved (fraud, lying). Someone on the Board maintained that this fact could not be taken into account, because competitive examinations exist only to verify aptitudes and knowledge, that is: what is of the intellectual order, but not what is of the moral order.

(This assertion, authorized by no legal provision, and evidently bad in its consequences, had been vaguely suggested by the fallacy discussed on page 59:

p. 203

cuando no se puede hacer todo, no hacer nada. En los concursos de oposición , no es posible, por lo general , investigar las condi-ciones morales , ni comprobar nada sobre ellas ; de aqui que se formara la opinión injustificada de que, aunque en algún caso se pudiera hacer eso .... etc.) . Muy a menudo se expresan opiniones como éstas : « ¿ Para qué cuidarse de que no se bese á los niños ! de todos modos , están expuestos á tantos miles de contagios ! ... sirvientas que se cambian á cada momento ; fruta ó ensalada que se compran, lavadas quién sabe con qué aguas ; bancos de los tranvias, etc. » . Valor de esos juicios : su carácter falacioso en tantos casos co-munes ( como, por ejemplo , el del texto) . Ejemplos de casos ó grados en que dejan de ser falaciosos . Párrafo de « La Moral Inglesa » de Guyau : << El principio fundamental, en la doctrina de la evolución, es la permanencia de la fuerza . En este punto coinciden la fisica y la psicologia. La fisica pende toda entera, según el señor Spencer, de ese principio esencial , sin el cual la mecánica no podria cons-tituirse . Por otra parte, la permanencia de la fuerza es también el principio primero de la psicologia, porque ella se reduce en último análisis á la permanenciacia de la conciencia. « La persistencia de la <<conciencia constituye la experiencia inmediata que tenemos de la << persistencia de la fuerza, y al mismo tiempo nos impone la nece- <<sidad en que estamos de afirmarla . » Como la conciencia moral , para el señor Spencer, no se distingue de la otra, se puede decir sin duda que la persistencia de la fuerza es el principio esencial de la moral , como es el de la psicología y de la fisica » . El profesor puede elegir y hacer analizar pasajes del género de éste, en que aparecen como agrandadas con vidrio de aumento las falacias tan comunes en los malos modes de filosofar : confusio-nes verbales y juegos de palabras : falsa precisión, falsa sistematiza-ción, simplicismo ; admisión fácil de proposiciones como si siem-pre debieran tener sentido , y debieran y pudieran ser ó afirmadas ó negadas , etc. A un bebedor de cerveza se le decía que el peligro de beber mucha era todavía mayor aqui que en Alemania, por la peor cali-dad de esa bebida. Respondió : « Al contrario ! La de aqui es más floja, y se puede beber más » . Uno de los presentes, intentó probarle que, si <<floja » queria

Very often opinions like these are expressed:

“Why take care that children are not kissed! in any case, they are exposed to so many thousands of contagions!... servants who change every moment; fruit or salad that is bought, washed in who knows what water; streetcar benches, etc.”.

Value of such judgments: their fallacious character in so many common cases (as, for example, that of the text). Examples of cases or degrees in which they cease to be fallacious.

Paragraph from Guyau’s “La Moral Inglesa”:

“The fundamental principle, in the doctrine of evolution, is the permanence of force. On this point physics and psychology coincide. Physics depends entirely, according to Mr. Spencer, on that essential principle, without which mechanics could not be constituted. On the other hand, the permanence of force is also the first principle of psychology, for psychology is reduced in the last analysis to the permanence of consciousness. ‘The persistence of consciousness constitutes the immediate experience we have of the persistence of force, and at the same time imposes on us the necessity under which we are of affirming it.’ As the moral consciousness, for Mr. Spencer, is not distinct from the other, one may no doubt say that the persistence of force is the essential principle of morals, as it is of psychology and of physics.”

The teacher can choose passages of this kind, and have them analyzed, in which the fallacies so common in bad ways of philosophizing appear as though enlarged under a magnifying glass: verbal confusions and plays on words: false precision, false systematization, simplism; easy admission of propositions as though they must always have a meaning, and must and could be either affirmed or denied, etc.

A beer drinker was being told that the danger of drinking a great deal of it was even greater here than in Germany, because of the poorer quality of that beverage. He answered: “On the contrary! Ours here is weaker, and one can drink more”.

One of those present tried to prove to him that, if “weak” meant

p. 204

decir algo, significaria la menor riqueza alcohólica ; pero que ese no era el mayor peligro , ó no era el solo , etc.; pero no lo enten-dían, y él parecia discutir mal. Preguntaba : « Pero ¿ qué entiende usted por « floja » ? » El otro , no entendía que no entendía; y, para los presentes, triunfo fácilmente en la discusión . Caso habitual, en que el que comprende mejor, es inhibido por la oscilación de sentido de los términos y tantas otras complica-ciones , mientras el que no comprende, discute con seguridad y ela-ridad aparente , etc. En el prólogo de « Los Apóstoles » de Renan, hay un párrafo (naturalmente , pensado en el plano excepcionalmente elevado en que lo está todo ese hermosísimo documento ) , muy indicado para hacer ver en él aquel sofisma estudiado en este libro, de razonar como si , ó se hubiera de obtener todo un efecto , ó no se hubiera de obtener nada, desconociendo así las acciones parciales , tenden-cias, etc. En una clase, convendria leerlo todo ( no lo transcribo aqui por brevedad , para que los estudiantes hicieran el esfuerzo por distinguir lo legitimo de lo falso . Empieza asi : <<El primer principio de la escuela critica, en efecto , es que cada uno admite en materia de fe lo que tiene necesidad de admitir, y hace, en cierto modo, el lecho de sus creencias proporcionado á su medida y á su talla. ¡ Cómo habríamos de ser lo bastante insensa-tos para intervenir en lo que depende de circunstancias sobre las cuales nadie puede nada ? Si alguien viene a nuestros principios , es que tiene la clase de mentalidad (tour d'esprit) y la educación necesarias para venir á ellos ; todos nuestros esfuerzos no darian esa educación y esa clase de mentalidad a quien no los tiene. » (También puede suministrar, ese párrafo , un ejemplo de esas argumentaciones separables de la tesis que defienden, que uno no está obligado á aceptar aunque acepte las tesis, y que cuando son, como ocurre en muchos casos , argumentaciones malas en fa-vor de tesis buenas, á veces comprometen esas tesis buenas , y, siempre , complican y perturban las discusiones y raciocinios ) . En los paises no muy perfectos desde el punto de vista electo-ral , ocurren todos los sofismas de grados ( lógicos , con proyeccio-nes morales ) à propósito de la legitimidad y pureza de las elec-ciones , y la posibilidad, para los ciudadanos honestos, de aceptar

anything, it would mean lower alcoholic content; but that this was not the greatest danger, or not the only one, etc.; but they did not understand him, and he seemed to argue badly.

He kept asking: “But what do you mean by ‘weak’?”

The other did not understand that he did not understand; and, in the eyes of those present, he triumphed easily in the discussion.

A habitual case, in which the one who understands better is inhibited by the oscillation in the meaning of the terms and so many other complications, while the one who does not understand argues with assurance and apparent clarity, etc.

In the preface to Renan’s “The Apostles” there is a paragraph (naturally, conceived on the exceptionally elevated plane on which that whole most beautiful document is conceived) that is very suitable for bringing out in it that sophism studied in this book, of reasoning as if either a whole effect must be obtained or nothing must be obtained, thus disregarding partial actions, tendencies, etc. In a class it would be advisable to read it all (I do not transcribe it here for brevity’s sake), so that the students might make the effort to distinguish what is legitimate from what is false.

It begins thus:

“The first principle of the critical school, in fact, is that each person admits in matters of faith what he has need to admit, and makes, in a certain way, the bed of his beliefs proportioned to his measure and his size. How could we be senseless enough to intervene in what depends on circumstances over which no one has any power? If someone comes to our principles, it is because he has the kind of mentality (tour d’esprit) and the education necessary to come to them; all our efforts would not give that education and that kind of mentality to one who does not have them.”

(That paragraph can also furnish an example of those arguments that are separable from the thesis they defend, which one is not obliged to accept even though one accepts the thesis, and which, when they are, as happens in many cases, bad arguments in favor of good theses, sometimes compromise those good theses, and always complicate and disturb discussions and reasonings.)

In countries not very perfect from the electoral point of view, all the sophisms of degree (logical ones, with moral projections) occur regarding the legitimacy and purity of elections, and the possibility, for honest citizens, of accepting

p. 205

diputaciones . Por ejemplo : no aceptar nunca, porque las condicio-nes de pureza teórica no son perfectas; aceptar siempre, porque «de todos modos, la fuerza electoral es un mito en estos paises , de manera que, si se quiere servir al pais ... » , etc. Falsa oposición en la teoria de Tolstoy sobre la guerra, cuando dice que los generales no tienen ninguna influencia en el éxito de de las batallas, y que su voluntad , sus órdenes , no producen nin-gún efecto (consecuencia que él ha sacado de que no sean, esos factores , los únicos que influyen, ó de que no influyan tanto como generalmente se supone ). En un Consejo Escolar, un miembro se había opuesto á que se destituyera à una maestra por supuestos desarreglos de conducta que parecían bastante verosimiles , pero que no estaban probados . Habiéndose opuesto ese mismo miembro , en otro momento, á que se nombrara para puesto análogo á una maestra que tenia mala re-putación, se le reprochó inconsecuencia . Se discute sobre la herencia de la tuberculosis : unos la niegan , otros la afirman . Uno de los primeros , enuncia la afirmación hoy acreditada por muchos médicos : « no se hereda la tuberculosis ( salvo casos muy excepcionales) ; lo que se hereda es el terreno » . ¡ Pues , heredar el terreno, es heredar la tuberculosis ! ( La cuestión, en ese momento , se hace de palabras ) . Oponiame un dia a que se transfirieran ciertos exámenes de No- . viembre á Diciembre ( me basaba en el calor) . Se me objetó : « De todos modos ( por las ocupaciones de los empleados, clases atrasa-das , etc. ) , nunca podemos empezar los exámenes el primer día de Noviembre, como está dispuesto en el Reglamento ... «Hugo es un árbol ; Racine una estaca » . (De Aug. Vacquerie : falsa oposición) . 14.

seats as deputies. For example: never to accept, because the conditions of theoretical purity are not perfect; always to accept, because “in any case, electoral force is a myth in these countries, so that, if one wishes to serve the country...”, etc.

False opposition in Tolstoy’s theory of war, when he says that generals have no influence on the outcome of battles, and that their will, their orders, produce no effect whatever (a consequence he has drawn from the fact that these factors are not the only ones that have influence, or that they do not have as much influence as is generally supposed).

On a School Board, a member had opposed the dismissal of a schoolmistress for alleged irregularities of conduct which seemed quite likely, but which were not proved. When this same member, on another occasion, opposed the appointment to a similar post of a schoolmistress who had a bad reputation, he was reproached with inconsistency.

The heredity of tuberculosis is being discussed: some deny it, others affirm it.

One of the former states the assertion now accepted by many physicians: “tuberculosis is not inherited (save in very exceptional cases); what is inherited is the terrain”.

—Well, to inherit the terrain is to inherit tuberculosis!

(The question, at that moment, becomes one of words.)

One day I was opposing the transfer of certain examinations from November to December (I based my opposition on the heat). The objection was made to me: “In any case (because of the employees’ occupations, classes that have fallen behind, etc.), we can never begin the examinations on the first day of November, as the Regulations prescribe...

“Hugo is a tree; Racine a stake”.

(From Aug. Vacquerie: false opposition.)

p. 206

Es muy interesante y útil mostrar á los estudiantes ejemplos en gran número de la falsa importancia que toman las cuestiones de palabras cuando se las toma erróneamente por de hecho : y cómo los hombres se obstinan en discutir si los hechos , cosas ó personas son ó no tal cosa, sin que esto quiera decir nada fuera de si se les debe ó no aplicar tal nombre ; pues , en el supuesto de que habla-mos , se conocen los hechos , y todos están de acuerdo sobre ellos . Por ejemplo : en nuestra politica se discute sobre si opinar que no se deben dar ministerios al partido opositor, es ó no ( aislando esa cuestión de toda otra) hacer « gobierno de partido » , ó sobre si esa ú otras prácticas son ó no compatibles con una « politica de coparticipación » . En realidad, esa práctica es eso lo que es : con sus ventajas ó inconvenientes . Estas ventajas é inconvenientes podrán, y deberán , discutirse ; pero lo falacioso es mezclar la cues-tión de palabras ( que, en este caso, despierta ideas y sentimientos apasionantes : ese agregado puramente verbal, ha podido influir hasta en la producción de alguna guerra civil ) . El sometimiento de tal revolución ¿ fué ó no « incondicional » ? Eso se discutió una vez con gran acritud y pasión , aún después de haberse publicado los documentos , y saberse, por consiguiente, cómo había sido de hecho el sometimiento . Sin duda, era cuestión importante, pero sólo de palabras . En ese caso , lo mejor era eludir la cuestión de palabras : ¿ cómo fué el sometimiento ? Fué así como fué: como está en esos documentos .... Dos novios riñen y rompen sus relaciones. Cuando narran el hecho , cuentan los hechos de la misma ma-nera ; pero , según la novia, es ella quien « dejó al novio ; y, se-gún él , al contrario. Esta diferencia no es, tal vez , puramente de palabras ; pero si en parte . La discusión que se sostiene en nuestra Universidad desde hace tantos años sobre si la teoria literaria debe enseñarse antes , ó des-pués, que la historia literaria, será un buen ejemplo ( si el profe-sor informa de ella á los estudiantes ) de mal planteamiento de una cuestión normativa.

It is very interesting and useful to show the students examples, in great number, of the false importance that questions of words take on when they are mistakenly taken for questions of fact: and how men persist in disputing whether facts, things, or persons are or are not such-and-such a thing, without this meaning anything beyond whether such-and-such a name should or should not be applied to them; for, in the case we are speaking of, the facts are known, and everyone is in agreement about them.

For example: in our politics it is disputed whether to hold that ministries ought not to be given to the opposition party is or is not (isolating that question from every other) to practice “party government,” or whether this or other practices are or are not compatible with a “policy of coparticipation.” In reality, that practice is that; what it is: with its advantages or drawbacks. These advantages and drawbacks may, and should, be discussed; but what is fallacious is to mix in the question of words (which, in this case, awakens ideas and feelings that arouse passion: that purely verbal addition may have had some influence even in bringing about a civil war).

Was the submission of such-and-such a revolution “unconditional” or not? This was once disputed with great acrimony and passion, even after the documents had been published, and it was consequently known how the submission had in fact taken place. No doubt it was an important question, but only one of words. In that case, the best thing was to evade the question of words: how did the submission take place? It took place thus; as it took place: as it stands in those documents....

Two sweethearts quarrel and break off their engagement.

When they relate what happened, they tell the facts in the same way; but, according to the girl, it is she who “left” her sweetheart; and, according to him, the contrary. This difference is not, perhaps, purely one of words; but it is so in part.

The discussion that has been carried on in our University for so many years as to whether literary theory should be taught before, or after, literary history, will be a good example (if the teacher informs the students about it) of the bad framing of a normative question.

p. 207

La teoría llamada « materialismo histórico > , < materialismo eco-nómico » , etc .: falsa oposición . En un estudio mio : « Contribución al estudio de la percepción métrica , publicado en mi obra « Ideas y Observaciones » ( Monte-video , Barreiro , 1905 ) , hay un ejemplo de falsa sistematización , muy aparente para ser aualizado con provecho en una clase. En ciertos pasajes de ese estudio , procuro probar que la acentuación de cada clase de verso , en los distintos idiomas, tiende å fijarse . La demostración que alli doy , es exacta, me parece, y los ejem-plos son apropiados para la mayor parte de los idiomas ; pero , con respecto al francés , como yo no sabia bien entonces que , en ese idioma, no hay propiamente acentos como en los otros, hago una demostración en falso , que sólo pudo producirse por la tendencia á sistematizar . En unos examenes de derecho , of discutir sobre si el matrimo-nio es ó no un contrato . Se discutia como si tuviera, ó que ser , ó que no ser, un contrato como los otros ( esto es : con los caracte-res de los otros , ó con los teóricos del contrato en general ). Entre las teorías que se mencionaron , estaba la « conciliatoria de que el matrimonio es « un contrato sui generis » ; pero un examinador eliminó de la discusión esa teoria, puesto que « el que haya que Alamarle sui-generis , es la mejor prueba de que no es contrato » . ( Cuestiones verbales ; espíritu verbalista y poco real ; mal uso de clasificaciones , y falacias ideo-verbales , etc. ) Decía un criador de aves : <<Casi mato el gallo de cría que me regalaron , por un error: Lo habia puesto dos veces con una gallina, y las dos veces salieron todos los huevos claros . Como la gallina era joven y sana, y como el gallo es viejo, y ha estado enfermo , creí que él tuviera la culpa. Por suerte se me ocurrió hacer una experiencia : lo echo con otras gallinas, y resulta que la culpa era de la gallina anterior : el gallo es notable para cria; un reproduc-tor excepcional : no falla un solo huevo ! >> Se puede afirmar casi con seguridad que la última afirmación es exagerada : el gallo será simplemente un reproductor como todos . Ver si el estudiante comprende la psicologia de eso : cómo vienen

The theory called “historical materialism,” “economic materialism,” etc.: false opposition.

In a study of mine, “Contribución al estudio de la percepción métrica,” published in my book “Ideas y Observaciones” (Montevideo, Barreiro, 1905), there is an example of false systematization, very suitable for profitable analysis in a class. In certain passages of that study, I try to prove that the accentuation of each kind of verse, in the different languages, tends to become fixed. The demonstration I give there is correct, it seems to me, and the examples are appropriate for most languages; but, with respect to French, since I did not then know well that in that language there are not, properly speaking, accents as in the others, I make a false demonstration, which could only have come about through the tendency to systematize.

At some law examinations, I heard a discussion as to whether marriage is or is not a contract. It was discussed as if it had either to be, or not to be, a contract like the others (that is: with the characteristics of the others, or with the theoretical ones of contract in general). Among the theories mentioned was the “conciliatory” one that marriage is “a contract sui generis”; but one examiner eliminated that theory from the discussion, since “the fact that it has to be called sui generis is the best proof that it is not a contract.” (Verbal questions; a verbalist and unrealistic spirit; bad use of classifications, and ideo-verbal fallacies, etc.)

A poultry breeder said: “I nearly killed the breeding rooster I was given, through a mistake: I had put him twice with a hen, and both times all the eggs came out infertile. Since the hen was young and healthy, and since the rooster is old, and has been sick, I thought he must be to blame. Fortunately it occurred to me to make an experiment: I put him with other hens, and it turns out that the fault lay with the earlier hen: the rooster is remarkable for breeding; an exceptional sire: not a single egg fails!”

It can be asserted almost with certainty that the last statement is exaggerated: the rooster is probably simply a sire like all the others. See whether the student understands the psychology of this: how

esas exageraciones ; cómo se producen en casos más importan-tes , etc. No , seguramente, para toda una clase por lo menos, para una clase de Enseñanza Secundaria), pero si para ciertos alumnos , y . no sólo para hacer lógica viva, sino para ayudar a otros fines de la enseñanza filosófica , el profesor debe llevar á la clase libros , revistas , memorias , etc., y analizarlos ó hacerlos analizar, no se-parando, aislando, los trozos que contengan falacias, sino hacién-dolas descubrir y sentir como están. Not, certainly, for a whole class (at least, for a class in Secondary Education), but yes for certain pupils, and not only in order to do living logic, but to help other ends of philosophical teaching, the teacher should bring books, reviews, memoirs, etc., to the class, and analyze them or have them analyzed, not separating, isolating, the passages that contain fallacies, but having the fallacies discovered and felt as they stand. I should never tire of repeating that this is the best method, and that if I, here, present the fallacious passages isolated, it is only for lack of space. But I wish to show a single case. Let the teacher bring to class, for example, the last two years of the Revue Philosophique (1909- 10), and he will be able to make observations such as these:

(Whoever reads those three lines will understand all the advantage a good teacher can draw from passages like that one, in order to make the good philosophical spirit felt, in opposition to simplistic and verbalist ways of philosophizing.)
